<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for alchemical "multiplication," transitioning from the Red Elixir (Gold) to the White Elixir (Silver), and providing the philosophical justification for why these substances possess the power to transform and increase themselves.</meta>

->The Work of the King is completed in itself; if you possess it and are a diligent guardian, then rejoice in God.<-

182. The order and method for composing and perfecting the **White Elixir** <note>the alchemical substance used to transmute base metals into silver, as opposed to the Red Elixir used for gold</note> are the same as previously described, provided that only white substances are used in its composition. Its bodies are brought to the final point of **decoction** <note>a process of boiling or heating a substance in a liquid to extract its essence or change its state</note> until it results in a white, shining, and almost crystalline plate. Once this is established with its own "white oil," it acquires the quality of fusion <note>the ability to melt and join with other metals</note>. Cast one weight of either elixir upon ten weights of washed **quicksilver** <note>original: "argenti vivi"; mercury</note>, and you will behold a wonderful result.

183. Since the nature of fire is manifested within the elixir, and the spirits of the **quintessence** <note>original: "quintae essentiae"; the purest and most concentrated essence of a substance, regarded as the "fifth element" beyond earth, air, fire, and water</note> are most freely multiplied, the corrupt "accidents" <note>in Aristotelian terms, these are the non-essential or outward qualities that can be changed without altering the core substance</note> of the bodies—which besieged their purity and the true light of nature with darkness—are removed through long and repeated **sublimations** <note>the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then becomes solid again to purify it</note> and **digestions** <note>the slow heating of a substance in a sealed vessel, often mimicking the warmth of a womb or the earth</note>. In this state, the nature of fire is liberated from its chains and, strengthened by the help of the heavenly powers, acts most powerfully when enclosed in this, our "fifth element." Therefore, it is no wonder if it obtains the power not only to perfect imperfect things <note>i.e., transforming base metals into noble ones</note>, but also to multiply its own strength. The source of this multiplication, however, lies in the "Prince of Lights" <note>a reference to the Sun, which in alchemy represents both the source of physical life and the spiritual/metallic principle of gold</note>, who, through the infinite multiplication of his rays, governs everything in this world of ours...

<summary>This page details the preparation of the White Elixir for silver-making and explains the theory of the Quintessence, arguing that the Elixir's power comes from liberating the "nature of fire" from material impurities.</summary>
<keywords>White Elixir, Quicksilver, Quintessence, Sublimation, Digestion, Alchemical Theory, Solar Principle</keywords>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for alchemical "multiplication," following the theoretical justification from the previous page. While the previous section touched upon the White Elixir, this text focuses specifically on the "multiplication" of the Red Elixir—the process of increasing the quantity and potency of the philosopher's stone so that a small amount can transform a vast quantity of base metal into gold.</meta>

...the multitude multiplied in stages increases its multiplicative power, excelling in its likeness to similar things.

134. There are three ways of multiplying the **Elixir**. In the first method: Take one weight of the **Red Elixir**, which you should mix with nine weights of its "red water" <note>original: "aqua sua rubra"; a specialized alchemical solvent, often a prepared mercurial liquid, used to dissolve the stone</note>. Dissolve this into a liquid in a **dissolving vessel** <note>a glass container designed for the "solutio" or dissolving stage of the work</note>. Soon, once it is perfectly dissolved and united, solidify it by simmering it over a slow fire until it hardens into a ruby or a red plate. Afterward, treat <note>original: "rurba"; likely a shorthand or error for "irrora" (moisten) or "rubifica" (redden)</note> this plate with its **red oil** in the manner previously taught until it flows <note>reaches a state where it melts like wax without evaporating, a sign of a successful "medicine"</note>. In this way, you will have a medicine ten times more powerful than the first. This task is completed easily and in a short time.

135. In the second method: Take a portion of your Elixir as you desire, mixed with its water. Keeping the correct proportions, seal it well in a dissolving vessel and dissolve it by **inhumation in a bath** <note>original: "inhumationem in balneo"; the practice of burying the vessel in a warm medium, such as a water bath or warm horse manure, to provide a steady, gentle heat over a long period</note>. Once dissolved, distill it, and by working the residue through the proper stages of fire, "fix" it <note>the process of turning a volatile substance into a stable solid that can resist the heat of fire</note>—just as was done in the first and second stages of the work—until it turns to stone. Finally, moisten it and cast it forth <note>original: "proice"; a reference to "projection," the final act of throwing the Elixir onto molten base metal to transform it</note>. This path is longer, but much more rewarding, for the power of the Elixir is increased a hundredfold. For the more refined it becomes through repeated operations, the more it retains the **powers of the above and the below** <note>original: "virium superiorum et inferiorum"; a core alchemical concept from the Emerald Tablet, suggesting the stone captures both celestial/spiritual and terrestrial/material energies</note> and operates with greater strength.

<summary>This page details two specific recipes for "multiplying" the Red Elixir, explaining how to increase its transformative power ten-fold or even a hundred-fold through repeated dissolution and solidification. The text emphasizes the use of "red water," "red oil," and controlled heating methods like the warm bath (inhumation).</summary>
<keywords>Red Elixir, multiplication, Red Water, Red Oil, dissolution, fixation, inhumation, projection, alchemical potency</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the instructions for the multiplication of the elixir, moving from the technical measurements of the "White" and "Red" works to the symbolic conclusion of the Great Work. 136. Finally, Master Oniary <note>original: "M. Oniary"; likely a reference to an obscure or pseudonymic alchemical authority</note> says to take one part, multiplied by the power of the spirit, and cast it over a hundred parts of the washed material <note>original: "Abluti"; refers to the purified base or "body" of the metal</note>. In a short time, it will become **mercury**; first, when heated, it will be converted into the purest water. If you cast any amount of this [Oniary] over another hundred similar parts of mercury, the purest **gold** <note>original: "sol"; literally "the Sun," the standard alchemical symbol for gold</note> will appear before your eyes, and thus the multiplication of the **white work** <note>original: "albi"; the stage of the work that produces silver</note> would be achieved. This being done, the power of this medicinal water for curing all diseases and preserving health is made clear in the practical use and writings of **Arnold of Villanova**, **Lully** <note>Raymond Lully, a major figure in medieval alchemy and logic</note>, as well as other authors of **Alchemy**.

137. For those seeking the proper timing of the **Stone**, the following will teach you: first, the **Moon** <note>silver</note> performs the work for the **White Elixir**; second, it must be finished by the "second water" of mercury. It is said, however, that the first work toward the **Red Elixir** is achieved by the second water through the power of the Sun <note>original: "virtute Dominicali"; literally "Lordly power," frequently used to refer to the Sun or Sunday in an alchemical context</note>. The final stage will end with the "white Royal water" of **Jupiter and the Sun**, from which our most powerful **King** <note>the perfected Red Stone or gold</note> shall receive a most precious, woven red crown.

->Thus the year is saved by following its own footsteps<-
<note>This centered phrase is a common alchemical motto suggesting that the process is cyclical, mirroring the natural cycles of the year.</note>

138. The Crime <note>original: "Scelus"; though it means crime or wickedness, in this context it may refer to the "killing" or mortification of the base metals</note>. This triple nature guards the **Dragon**: the first head comes from the waters, the second from the earth, and the third from the air; indeed, these three heads shall end in one most powerful being. It is necessary that the **Universal Solvent** <note>original: "Catholicos"; likely referring to the *Alkahest* or the universal medicine</note> shall devour the Dragon; then the way to the **Kingdom** is opened to you. Farewell, friend reader. In reading these things, cast away the dark hope of light <note>perhaps an injunction to seek true spiritual illumination rather than false promises</note>. I speak few words, but much is prophesied. Farewell. Judge for yourself.

->the end<-

<meta>This page transitions from the general techniques of multiplication discussed on the previous page to the specific astrological timing and symbolic progression of the Great Work. It explains how the alchemical process mirrors the movement of the sun and planets through the zodiac, starting with the "winter" of the work under Saturn and ending with its perfection.</meta>

-> # The Meaning of the Signs and the Houses of the Planets <-

<image-desc>A square astrological chart divided into twelve triangular houses. The central square is blank. The outer triangles contain zodiac symbols and planetary symbols. The labels around the square are translated as: "Northern Signs, of summer" (top), "Eastern signs, of spring" (left), "Southern signs, of winter" (bottom), and "Western signs, of autumn" (right). The symbols include:
- Aries and Scorpio paired with **Mars**
- Taurus and Libra paired with **Venus**
- Gemini and Virgo paired with **Mercury**
- Cancer paired with the **Moon**
- Leo paired with the **Sun**
- Sagittarius and Pisces paired with **Jupiter**
- Capricorn and Aquarius paired with **Saturn**</image-desc>

The figure described here is the **Zodiac** <note>original: "Signifer"; literally the 'sign-bearer,' a common term for the zodiac belt</note> of the philosophers. A double house <note>original: "domicilium"; in astrology, a 'house' is a zodiac sign ruled by a specific planet</note> was designated for each planet by the ancients, except for the **Sun** and the **Moon**, each of whom is allotted only a single house. According to the proximity in the said figure, each planet occupies its own houses.

<margin>
**Aries** — ♈︎ Equinox of **Mars**, the first beginning.
**Taurus** — ♉︎
**Gemini** — ♊︎
**Cancer** — ♋︎ Thus the summer tropic; here the **Sun** is at its highest above our horizon.
**Leo** — ♌︎
**Virgo** — ♍︎
**Libra** — ♎︎ [Equinox] of September.
**Scorpio** — ♏︎
**Sagittarius** — ♐︎
**Capricorn** — ♑︎ This is the winter tropic; here the **Sun** is at its lowest and furthest from our horizon.
**Aquarius** — ♒︎
**Pisces** — ♓︎
</margin>

Where the Philosophers, in treating their <insert>philosophical</insert> year, begin their year from the winter—specifically from **Capricorn**, as **Lully** <note>Raymond Lully, the influential alchemist mentioned on the previous page</note> teaches in chapter 36 of the *Magisterium*. This is the first house of **Saturn**. Moving forward from there, the second house of Saturn occurs in the sign of **Aquarius**, at which time **Saturn** or the "blackness" <note>the *Nigredo* stage, characterized by rot and decomposition</note> of the work begins to dominate. After approximately 45 days, with the Sun entering **Pisces**, the work becomes "blacker than black," and the **Crow’s Head** <note>original: "caput corvi"; a famous alchemical symbol for the deepest point of the Nigredo stage</note> begins to appear. 

When the third month is completed and the Sun enters **Aries**, the **sublimation** <note>the process of vaporizing and condensing a substance to purify it</note> or the separation of the elements begins. The following stages, up to **Cancer**, serve to whiten the work <note>the *Albedo* or white stage, which produces the silver-making elixir</note>. In **Cancer**, brightness and splendor are added as they arrive, and then the stone is said to be made of **white sulfur**, or the work of the **Moon** <note>original: "Lunaria"; relating to the silver stage</note> is fulfilled by the sulfur, especially while the Moon sits and reigns in her glorious house. 

In **Leo**, the "Palace of the Sun," the **solar work** begins, which is terminated in **Libra** as the **red stone** or **perfect sulfur** <note>the *Rubedo* or red stage, the final perfection that produces gold</note>. The two remaining signs, **Scorpio** and **Sagittarius**, are said to be the completion. And so, with **Saturn** marking the beginning and **Jupiter** reigning at the end, the offspring of the philosophers <note>the "Philosopher's Stone"</note> assumes its wonders and perfection.

<term>Signifer</term> — The Zodiac belt.
<term>Domicilium</term> — A "house" or zodiac sign ruled by a planet.
<term>Saturnus</term> — Saturn, associated with lead and the "black" stage of decay.
<term>Caput Corvi</term> — The Crow's Head, symbolizing the successful completion of the Nigredo.
<term>Sublimation</term> — A technical process of purifying a solid by turning it into vapor.
<term>Opus Solare</term> — The Solar Work, the final stage of making gold.
<term>Sulfur</term> — One of the three primary alchemical principles, representing the active, masculine, or combustible force.
<term>Lullius</term> — Raymond Lully, a 13th-century polymath and supposed alchemist.

It highlights Saturn as the ruler of the beginning of the work in winter and Jupiter as the ruler of its final perfection.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the practical instructions attributed to Raymond Lull, specifically focusing on the definitions and the initial stages of the alchemical process. The catchword at the bottom right is partially lost due to the manuscript's condition.</meta>

->The Practice of the Old Testament<-
<note>original: "Practica Antiqui testamenti". This title refers to one of the most famous alchemical works attributed to Lull, though likely written by his followers.</note>

Here begins the practical manual of Master Raymond Lull regarding the Philosophers' Stone, beginning with the definition of what it is, and what the science or art of Alchemy represents.

### Chapter 1.

Alchemy is a hidden branch of natural philosophy—and a most necessary one—which is established as an art and science that is not known to all. It teaches how to cleanse and purify all precious stones that are imperfect or deficient in weight, bringing them to their proper balance <term>temperamentum</term><note>The "temperament" or balance of the four primary qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note>. It provides the way by which fallen and weak bodies may be restored and returned to their proper balance for optimal health. Furthermore, it teaches how to transmute all metallic bodies into "the Moon" <note>silver</note> and "the Sun" <note>gold</note>, and eventually into the Sun alone. This is achieved through a single medicinal body into which all specific medicines are reduced and unified. This process is completed and performed through a manual method <note>regimen</note> understood by the "perfect sons of philosophy" <note>a traditional term for initiated alchemists</note>, using the "water of heat" in relation to the six degrees of warmth which are central to this work.

The first degree of heat is called "the subdued" <note>original: "hebetatus"</note>; it hinders and guards against the natural integral movement. Therefore, it leads to and induces "putrefaction" <note>the stage of decomposition or blackening</note> in mixed substances. Without this decay, the true spirit cannot remain within the matter.

The second heat is "the excited" heat, which provides a tolerable warmth for vivification <note>bringing the matter back to "life" after its symbolic death</note>. This heat leads and...

<summary>This page introduces the definition of alchemy as a medicinal and transformative art capable of purifying stones and metals. It begins detailing the different degrees of heat required for the alchemical "Great Work," starting with the stages of decay and revitalization.</summary>
<keywords>Raymond Lull, alchemy, Philosophers' Stone, transmutation, gold and silver, putrefaction, degrees of heat, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the degrees of heat from the previous page, specifically detailing the third, destructive degree, and transitioning into the "Royal Flower" of alchemical science. Ink bleed-through is visible from the reverse side at the top and bottom of the leaf.</meta>

It leads to generations and multiplications. The third is excessive and deadly heat which destroys all senses and instruments, instincts, and natural appetites; through this, corruptions, generations, and multiplications occur. Specifically, this science is called the **Royal Flower** <term>flora regalis</term><note>original: "flora regalis". Likely a reference to the 'flos chemicus' or the 'flower of the kings,' symbolizing the highest attainment of the alchemical art.</note>, through which the human intellect is strengthened by experience, visual observation, and true knowledge. Because of this, its experience cannot admit any fanciful proof <note>original: "probationem phantasticam"; refers to deceptive or imaginary results versus physical, repeatable proof.</note>, but instead shows the intellect how to enter all other sciences by divine means—how it possesses the divine power <term>virtutem divinam</term> to penetrate those things which are meant to be hidden.

Thus, through nature, we understand that which truly exists, whereas many fools and madmen believe that there is nothing there. But we know if this thing is so because we know it through faith; it is not called "known" by the ignorant. For through such science, the intellect is purged of superfluities <note>meaning unnecessary or distracting thoughts/impurities</note> which remove it from the whole truth. In another way, this science is named "concerning the vigils and instruments of the spirit," through which good rulers and ancient poets directed their senses toward every kind of science, to enter into all experience through the art of knowing, according to the natural course and through real knowledge.

### Chapter 2.

My son, in this second practical part, you must know...

It is our intention to deliver the practice, and the method of practicing with a double Instrument.

The first Instrument is the mnemonic practice <note>original: "memorativa practica"; referring to the "Art of Memory," a system used to organize and recall vast amounts of information using mental images or physical diagrams</note>, or the Recordative and Revolutive <note>original: "Recordativa, et Revolutiva"; likely referring to the use of volvelles or revolving paper wheels used in medieval science to calculate relationships between concepts</note>.

The second is the operative practice, where everything is mixed together simultaneously. This is done successively, through the mode and form of an alphabet, according to the determination of each <term>definition</term> <note>original: "diffinitio"; here meaning the specific essence or boundary of an alchemical concept</note>. You should understand this to be from the mind of the Royal Mastery <note>original: "Regalis magisterium"; the supreme work or "Great Work" of alchemy</note>, and you must form it through art in order to perform and achieve a "practiced-fact" which is certain. We shall show this to shine before your eyes by an action known to you through experience.

Because the virtue of the beginning corresponds to the progression toward the end, and there can be no end except by a middle virtue which acts as a link between the two extremities, this proceeds from the reason I have stated: that we should formalize the aforementioned Instrument through a numerical impulse <note>original: "numerativam elateri"; likely referring to a mathematical or rhythmic drive that powers the logical "machine" of the instrument</note>. This is especially true as it has been dictated to us, for <unclear>Adalia</unclear> shows us that understood theory <note>original: "theorica intellecta"</note> must not be separated at any step, nor distanced from known practice <note>original: "practica scita"</note>. Indeed, one forms the other and corrects its defects. Therefore, we have placed the mnemonic practice according to this science in a middle form, because naturally

<summary>The author introduces two "instruments" for alchemical practice: one for memory and calculation (mnemonic and revolving) and one for physical operation (alphabetical and sequential). He emphasizes that theory and practice must never be separated, as they mutually refine and correct one another.</summary>

...the end of its own proper eternities <note>original: "eternitatibus"; in this context, it likely refers to the timeless, unchanging principles or archetypal states that the alchemist seeks to reach</note>, and its own proper eternities are the operative practice on the second side, while on the first side is its sister, namely theory. Outside of these principles, the science is formed through the first [theory], because it is never said to be a true science unless it is first founded and begun from its own certain principles.

### Concerning the second division of this <unclear>second</unclear> part through the mnemonic instrument

### Chapter 3.

This Book, which is called and named the second part, is divided and partitioned into four principal operations. The first is to dissolve, the second is to wash <note>original: "abluere"; the process of purification or "cleansing" the matter of its impurities</note>, the third is to congeal, and the fourth is to fix the <term>maam</term> <note>A technical term likely derived from "materia," referring to the process of "fixing" or stabilizing a volatile substance so it can withstand fire</note>. 

The first and most fundamental operation is divided into two dissolutive parts, just as we taught in our theory. Through the first, we create a union from a plurality <note>original: "de pluralitate unionem"; the act of combining various ingredients into a single substance</note>; through the second, we create a plurality from a union <note>original: "de unione pluralitatem"; the act of breaking down or analyzing a unified substance into its constituent parts</note>, just as it is shown to you through this honest and beautiful doctrine, my dearest son. And so, it is given to you to be gathered through certain and determined alphabetical distinctions—namely, by way of an alphabet of A, B, C, and of figures, definitions, mixtures, and applications.

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the mnemonic instrument's symbolic system, moving from the general four-stage process discussed in Chapter 3 to the specific meanings of the "alphabet" (A, B, C) as applied to the first operation. The focus here is on the theological origin of the work and the multifaceted nature of Mercury.</meta>

### Concerning the first distinction, which is of A, B, C, etc.
### Chapter 4

Regarding the first distinction, which is A, B, and C: In the first operation, **A** signifies God our Lord, who created all things and from whom all things proceed. **B** signifies Quicksilver <note>original: "Argentum vivum"; literally "living silver," the common name for Mercury, the most essential substance in the alchemist’s laboratory</note>, which is a celestial substance even in its state as a corruptible body, as is apparent through its properties. For it possesses the action of corrupting and generating, of joining and dividing, of thickening, positioning, thinning, hardening, and softening; of increasing, decreasing, dissolving, coagulating, and calcining <note>original: "Calcinandi"; the process of reducing a substance to a powder by heat</note>; of mortifying <note>original: "mortificandi"; the "killing" or chemical breakdown of a substance</note>, vivifying <note>bringing back to "life" or reactivity</note>, making raw, and ripening; of washing, drying, moistening, heating, and cooling; of smoothing, closing, making rough, sweetening, and making bitter; of strengthening and weakening; of entering, making ingressions <note>original: "Ingressiones"; the ability of a substance to penetrate and transform the interior of another metal</note>, shaping, and giving weight; of troubling, feeding, impregnating, fattening, and sublimating <note>original: "sublimandi"; the process where a solid turns into vapor and then back to solid, used for purification</note>. It is also used for creating the **First Matter** <note>original: "primam Materiam"; the formless, primordial substance required to begin the Great Work</note>, for the betrothal and the **First Marriage** <note>original: "primum Matrimonium"; the symbolic union of opposing principles, like Mercury and Sulfur</note>, and for blinding and igniting both bodies and spirits. Indeed, this Quicksilver knows the entire doctrine of the "son of the work of nature" <note>A common term for the alchemist or the "Philosopher's Stone" itself</note> by discerning all these properties from one another, as well as many others.

### On the second distinction, which concerns the figures of the first dissolving part

### Chapter 5

This figure teaches that by means of **A**, the six figures mentioned are composed for the first part of the **dissolving process** <note>original: "solutivæ"; the stage of the Great Work focused on breaking down solid substances into a liquid state</note>, namely: three triangular figures and three circular figures.

The first circular figure is represented by **E**, and is formed from **b, C, and d**. The second circular figure is represented by **I**, and is formed from the second composite triangle of **E, f, and g**. The third circular figure is represented by **K**, and is formed from the third composite triangle of **E, g, and h**. This figure pertains only to the first dissolving part, which is more properly called the <term>Liquefying phase</term> <note>original: "Liquefactiva"; the specific alchemical operation of turning a solid body into a fluid through heat or moisture</note>.

# On the Second Practical Instrument within the First, and on the First Dissolving Part

### Chapter 7

A **practical instrument** <note>original: "Instrumentum practicale"</note> is as much to say as putting theory into operational fact by means of an instrument—which, in this case, is the **memory**. Through this memory, I must show you how the letters mentioned are arranged into diagrams for the **first dissolving part** <term>pars solutiva: the initial stage of alchemy where a solid substance is broken down into its primary liquid form</term>. These consist of six primary figures. Once you have made such an instrument habitual to your mind, it is seen by converting it into practice by applying it to the first figure, which is composed of the letters **B.**, **C.**, and **D.**, in the following manner.

Practice and doctrine concerning how it ought to be done, my son, from two physical substances and one spiritual and active substance brought into the **house of the wind** <note>original: "domuz ventu" (domum venti); an alchemical allusion to the Emerald Tablet, referring to the volatile or gaseous state of the matter</note>, just as the intention will be represented to you, my son, by this figure—

If you see that it turns from fifteen points to twelve or less, strengthen the fire and continue according to the point of its distillation. And afterwards, for a third time, strengthen your fire by one point more and continue until nothing more distills. Then leave it, and to stop the fire, close your furnace.

### On the first dissolving part through practical application, and on liquefaction
### Chapter 10.

Holy son of the world, it is now necessary to dissolve a part of it through the liquefaction of all its components—those that are essential <term>substantiales: the inner, unchanging core of a substance</term> and those that are accidental <term>accidentales: the outward, changeable qualities like color or texture</term>. Let these be protected during the grinding <term>trituratione: the process of reducing a solid to powder</term> performed by the movement of **fire against nature** <term>ignis contra naturam: a specialized alchemical heat, often referring to a corrosive chemical agent or a heat that works contrary to ordinary burning</term>. Because of this, foolish predecessors were ignorant of the proper measure; therefore, you shall proceed in this manner, without touching the matter with your feet or hands. As has been stated, take one ounce of **f.** <note>likely a shorthand for a specific material or 'form'</note>, well-purified by a hair-sieve or by strong ash.

But guard against excessive heat, for we have seen this process succeed even by the heat of the sun; again, proceed well, unless you wish to deceive yourself. 

Maintain this heat continuously for eleven hours, after which you should stop up the <unclear>furnace</unclear> and go to bed to sleep. You will see it working and the smoke ascending on high. 

Place the calcined metal from the <term>liquefaction</term>, but better than before, stop it up with its own lid that is made for the inside, and put it back well into another heat until it has been worked by its own internal virtue. After it has been worked, there follow the <term>sealings</term> <note>original: "luta"</note> which are <unclear>to be well made</unclear> with <unclear>egg</unclear> <unclear>white</unclear>.

### How the artist ought to possess the fixed earth
## Chapter 17

My son, after these separations, you will possess the <term>fixed earth</term> <note>original: "terram fixam"; a substance that has been purified until it can no longer be changed or vaporized by fire</note> in such a way that nothing from it can be <term>sublimated</term> <note>original: "sublimari"; the process where a substance is heated into vapor and then condensed back into solid form, used here to test the "fixity" of the earth</note> any further. It is necessary for you to know that the mercurial properties overcome the foul-smelling <term>menstrual properties</term> <note>original: "proprietates foetentes menstruales"; in alchemy, "menstrual" refers to the base solvent or "mother" substance, often described as foul or impure until properly refined</note>, if you wish to establish the foundation for the perfection of nature's precious being. My son, then <term>all</term> <note>original: "oẽs" for "omnes"</note> three properties of this term are given to you to recognize: namely the <term>matter</term> <note>original: "mãam" for "materiam"</note>, the power, and the end goal which has been declared in the property of this art. Therefore, because any property—though it may be only one—seems to speak to the mind and only within itself reaches its conclusion, it receives its termination from its own cause.

<meta>This page follows the discussion of the "fixed earth" and the transition from the "white work" to the "red work." Having established the physical residue of the process, the author now pivots to explaining the underlying theory of the four elements and why specific degrees of heat—the "four fires"—are required to manage their opposing natures.</meta>

<margin>
Properties of the
Elements
</margin>

Observable experience, illuminated by clear light, shows us effectively—following the path of dividing the four <term>Elements</term> <note>The basic building blocks of matter in medieval science: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note>—how their natures can be seen in a manifest way. Namely, the property found in the part of **earth** differs greatly in its ability to be <term>coagulated</term> <note>original: "coagulari"; the process of a liquid becoming solid or semi-solid.</note> and <term>fixed</term> <note>original: "fixari"; rendered resistant to the action of fire.</note>. That which is in the **water** draws apart, greatly dissolves, and washes. The property in the **air** moistens, wets, softens, and allows flow through every opening. Finally, that which is in the **fire** heats and <term>tints</term> <note>original: "tingit"; likely referring to the "tincture," the alchemical power to transform the color and quality of a metal.</note>.

To this end, these properties receive a further determination based on the matter of their specific Element, whether in its coarseness or its subtlety, through various changes. Because of these variations, there are **four principal Fires** to be observed, corresponding to the substance and properties of the four Elements. Therefore, the property found in the earth is of a coarser substance and a more fixed nature, allowing it to have a greater tolerance against a stronger fire. For this reason, a specific degree of Fire is properly owed to it according to the requirements of its nature, and the same applies to the other Elements.

From this, it is clear that while preserving its unique properties, matter is governed through certain exciting and stimulating **degrees of heat**. These separable Elements ensure that the matter is not weakened or stripped of its properties by excessive fire or coarseness, nor driven against its own nature. For without this care, those Elements are discordant and "mortal enemies" to the process of <term>generation</term> <note>The alchemical "birth" of the Stone or the transformation of the matter.</note>, unless **geometry** <note>original: "geometria"; here used to mean the precise mathematical proportion and balance of the ingredients.</note> and rule are strictly observed.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions following the discussion of the four elements. Apply the care with which you will be able to draw it out well, for without this <term>rule</term> <note>original: "regula"; referring to a specific procedure or axiom in the art.</note> you cannot proceed. My son, it must be known here that the <term>mill</term> <note>original: "molina"; in alchemical literature, this often refers to a vessel or a mechanical setup for continuous circulation, or metaphorically to the grinding/pulverizing of matter.</note> possesses every means to draw out the **water** which is full of its mediating property. 

My son, I will tell you what we have already said regarding how you will be able to extract it with the appropriate **fire**. But now we shall describe our entire **instrument** by which you will be able to guard and direct the **water** in its proper course. When you wish to draw it elsewhere from its own dwelling, it is necessary for you to make my work your own. 

My son, in the subject of <term>rarefaction</term> <note>original: "raritatia"; the state of being thin or less dense, as in a gas or subtle vapor.</note> there are extremes and means, according to "more" or "less," because of which one extreme flees into the other and is converted into it, so as to escape the necessity of nature. As we shall demonstrate to you more fully through the example of **water** in which as much **salt** is dissolved as the watery moisture can contain relative to the richness of the salt. 

Wherefore we show you that moisture is porous and full of holes. Within this **porosity** is contained the **salt**, which is a <term>natural stone</term> <note>original: "lapis nãalis"; here salt is described as a stone carried within the liquid medium.</note> carried in the water, because it resembles the stone existing in its own seat. My son, its "seats" are the pores and the small holes of its waters, which at first—while they were full of something very subtle—are afterwards filled with a substance that thickens itself. For this reason, it is clear that the more it is thickened, the more it flees **rarefaction** and moves toward **condensation** as the pores and small holes are filled.

<meta>This page continues the technical discourse on the behavior of elements. While the previous page focused on the "mill" and the relationship between water and salt in terms of porosity, this page shifts to a philosophical and physical defense of the impossibility of a vacuum, explaining how "gross" matter is replaced by more "subtle" substances like air and ether.</meta>

Gross **matter** becomes simpler through evacuation, because at no time does nature allow a vacuum to exist; it is always full of that which expands as much as the space can endure. We have demonstrated this in four ways, as well as through the opinion of the seekers of truth regarding <term>Hylirity</term> <note>original: "Hylirity"; likely a derivative of "Hyle," the Aristotelian term for primordial matter or substance without form.</note> and in our own work *On the Extractions of Natural Airs*. Wherefore some of the ancients speak of a "shrine," saying that a vacuum is granted by nature from every entity, which it attracts to itself from its own degree, yet without having the depth of true nature. 

Therefore, although the heat of **Fire** consumes the moist **matter** of the air and renders it porous, the said matter still occupies the stated place, notwithstanding that it attracts—according to the proportion of a vacuum—more to that place than it previously held. This is apparent in those recovering after a great **infirmity** <note>original: "Infirmitem Magnam"; referring to the period of convalescence after a serious illness.</note>, whose nature desires more than it can digest; and yet that proper place, according to its entity, never remains empty of all the entities of nature. 

Rather, it is only emptied of its more simple nature, which participates there according to our opinion of a quite simple and most simple sensibility, which are called the simplest units. Such is the **air** in which we breathe, with respect to its two extremes: namely, the element of simple **water** and the simplest pure **air**, which is called <term>aether</term> <note>original: "aether"; the "fifth element" or quintessence, thought to fill the universe beyond the terrestrial sphere.</note> by the **Philosophers**. Properly speaking, it exists after **Fire**, and if this were to be seen, no element would be visible. It possesses the sphere of animation and station, and especially that air called ether, which cannot be destroyed by **Fire** nor corrupted by the force of heat, for it is always so close to it and joined with it. Therefore, it is declared that a vacuum is never granted to nature without it being filled by the entity of the lower spheres, because **Fire** [finds] its place there...

The author uses the metaphor of a recovering patient's appetite to describe how nature "attracts" substance to fill any potential void.</summary>

Consequently, it is preserved without a **vacuum** <note>original: "sine vacua"; nature’s perceived "abhorrence" of empty space (horror vacui) was a central tenet of medieval physics, positing that a vacuum cannot exist in nature.</note>. Such a vacuum was never, or hardly ever, required by nature to be maintained without the aid of other powers.

Since it is thus—and as if from the very banks of existence—it always desires to reach and recover its perfection. It bestows an **attractive power**, drawing the substance of other elements to itself with a great **appetite** <note>original: "appetitu"; in scholastic philosophy, "appetite" refers to a natural tendency or inclination of an object toward its proper place or state.</note> through a process of **rarefaction** <term>rarefaction</term> <note>The process by which matter becomes less dense and expands to fill space.</note>. In this way, they reclaim what is owed to them from the matter. 

Specifically, it recovers that which it previously lost through the heat of the fire. The more it empties a place, the more it attracts the matter of those elements through its great appetite; it possesses greater forces for a greater attraction. As we have told you, this is visible in the **condensation** of air and in rain. It is seen that **vital nature**, in its restoration, strengthens and gives life to the limb—

<meta>This page continues the physical and cosmological argument against the existence of a vacuum. While the previous page discussed the nature of fire and its "sphere," this section provides specific proofs—such as the medical use of cupping glasses and the recovery from illness—to demonstrate that space is always filled by something, whether it be "gross matter," air, or the celestial "ether."</meta>

**Gross matter** <term>gross matter</term> <note>original: "materia grossa"; in medieval science, this refers to dense, tangible substances (like earth or water) compared to "subtle" substances (like air or light).</note> is removed simply through evacuation, because at no time is a **vacuum** <term>vacuum</term> <note>The "horror vacui" or "nature's abhorrence of a vacuum" was a cornerstone of physics before the 17th century; it was believed that the universe was a "plenum," or completely full.</note> permitted to exist, for the "fullness" is extended everywhere. Although matter can undergo changes, we have demonstrated this in four ways; this is known from the science of **cupping glasses** <note>original: "ventosorum"; a medical treatment where heated glass jars are placed on the skin to create suction. This was frequently used as a physical proof that air or humors move to prevent an empty space from forming.</note> and not only in the operations of natural acts.

Wherefore, some of the ancients seem to say that a vacuum is never granted to any entity when they attract one another from their own level, but rather by not having the natural depth of air. Therefore, although the heat of fire consumes the humid matter of the air and renders said matter porous, it still keeps that place full. This remains true despite the fact that it attracts more than it previously gave off, in proportion to the vacuum of that place. 

This is apparent in those **recovering from a great illness** <note>Medieval physicians believed that during illness, the body's "substance" was consumed. During recovery, the body "attracts" nourishment to fill the perceived void left by the disease.</note>, whose nature craves more than it can digest; and yet, in its very essence, that place never remains a vacuum to all natural entities. Instead, it is filled by its more simple Nature, which participates there quite simply according to our sensory knowledge. These most simple things are the more simple **elements**, such as the air in which we breathe. 

This pertains to its outer limit—namely, in respect to the element of simple water and the most simple, pure air, which is called **ether** <term>ether</term> <note>original: "aether"; the "fifth element" or quintessence. Philosophers believed it was the incorruptible substance that filled the heavens above the sphere of fire.</note> by the Philosophers. Properly, it exists beyond the sphere of fire. If this is observed, no element having a **sphere** <term>sphere</term> <note>In the Aristotelian model, the elements are arranged in concentric layers: Earth, Water, Air, and Fire.</note> possesses its own animation and station, especially in the air called ether. This ether cannot be destroyed by fire nor corrupted by its heat, even though fire is always near and joined to it. Therefore, it has been declared that a vacuum is never granted to nature, except perhaps regarding the entity in the lower spheres, because fire [takes its] place there...

...it possesses, just as nature possesses an appetite for its own perfection. In another way, the **nature of Fire** <note>original: "Ignis natura"; in medieval cosmology, Fire was the lightest element, existing in a "sphere" just below the moon.</note> is corrupted and moves through animate beings, which is a certain sphere for each of the elements, and [higher?], and nevertheless without power and without attraction; consequently, it moves through without a **vacuum** <term>vacuum</term> <note>original: "vacua"; a void or space containing nothing. Medieval science followed Aristotle's belief that "nature abhors a vacuum."</note> which was never at any time acquired by nature, which <gloss>and rather by spirit</gloss> it might pervade without those powers. Wherefore, with the **simpler matter** <term>simpler matter</term> <note>original: "materia simpliciore"; likely referring to air, which is more "subtle" and less dense than earth or water.</note> being consumed—namely the air which it generously yields in this sphere that was given to it and is properly owed to it by the way of **heaviness and lightness** <note>original: "ponderositatis et levitatis"; the Aristotelian theory that objects move up or down based on their inherent "weight" or "lightness" relative to their natural place.</note>—then, at all times and in every place, it preserves a simple [measure] insofar as the fire is distant, and they cannot consume it. 

But as for this simplest matter, since the said sphere is thus sufficient or sufficiently occupying and filling the place according to its substance, therefore nature—wishing in said place through its own instinct and institution to recover through restoration its lost matter with a stable and equal [desire]—is always desiring to reach its perfection and to recover. It gives into an **attractive power** <term>attractive power</term> <note>original: "attractiva virtute"; a magnetic-like force believed to draw matter together to prevent a gap in the universe.</note>, drawing to itself the substance of other elements with a great appetite through their **rarefaction** <term>rarefaction</term> <note>original: "rarefactionem"; the process of a substance becoming thinner or less dense, typically when heated.</note>. By this, nature recovers its due—namely that which it had lost through the heat of the Fire. 

And the more it evacuates a place, the more it attracts the nature of those elements through its great appetite, and it possesses a greater force of greater attraction, as we have told you. This appears in the **condensation of air** <term>condensation of air</term> <note>original: "condensatione aeris"; the process of air thickening or cooling, which was thought to pull other matter into the space it previously occupied.</note> and rain, and as it is seen when the **vital nature** <term>vital nature</term> <note>original: "natura vitalis"; the internal force of a living being that governs growth and healing.</note> in restoration is strengthening and vivifying a limb —

<summary>This page explains that nature uses an "attractive power" and "appetite" to pull in matter from surrounding elements to fill any space emptied by heat or consumption, ensuring a vacuum never forms. It compares this physical process to meteorological events like rain and the biological healing of a limb.</summary>

On the operation of the second <term>regimen: a specific stage or set of procedures in the alchemical process</term>, which is the washing <note>original: 'ablavatio'; the purification of the matter through liquid</note>

And first regarding water and air, which have the task of cleansing

### Chapter 18

Son, when you have divided the elements of the stone—or the stones—into the four elements, it is necessary that you purge them through this regimen. But first, the substance of the four elements must be understood and recognized; wherefore we tell you that earth and fire are of a stony substance, and therefore the preparation of a calcining fire <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder or 'ash'</term> is necessary.

The other two elements, namely water and air, are of a watery substance and an aquatic nature, and for that reason, it is necessary that their preparation be ordered to purge their nature. For air and water require a preparation which is performed through seven distillations or more, until they are free from all burning <note>original: 'adustione'; refers to the 'burnt' or carbonized impurities that contaminate the alchemical work</note>. Then, because it is spoken of as being cleansed of mortal sin <note>The author uses moral/religious language to describe the removal of chemical 'faults' or base impurities from the substance</note>, and then each of them exists by itself, the tincture <term>tincture: the concentrated essence or 'soul' of the matter used to transform other substances</term> will remain without <unclear>creature</unclear> and free from burning.

Son, you shall distill the water and the air separately, each in its own rectification <term>rectification: the refining of a liquid by repeated distillation to achieve higher purity</term>. And afterwards, you shall distill them by themselves, and later you shall place the waters with the earth, as you shall do in every distillation. After seven distillations, you shall place one or two drops upon a hot plate of fine and pure silver; if the silver turns black, the material is not yet free from its burning. Therefore, submit it to a seventh distillation and more, until it leaves the silver without <note>The text ends abruptly here, likely meaning 'without stain' or 'without discoloration'.</note>

<summary>This page describes the 'Second Regimen' of the alchemical process, focusing on the purification of the water and air elements through repeated distillation. ## On the Washing of Fire and Earth
### Chapter 19

Son, when you wish to wash the fire, you must first separate the water in which it rests through distillation <term>distillation: the process of heating a liquid to create vapor and then condensing it back into liquid to purify it</term>. Collect all the dregs <note>original: 'feces'; the solid residue or impurities left after a distillation process</note> that are colored between black and red and put them together. To these, add their own weight of the "first water," and then distill the whole until nothing remains but dry fire. You shall repeat this process seven times <note>The number seven frequently represents a cycle of perfection or spiritual completion in alchemy</note> until at last the fire is red and in the form of a fine powder. 

Son, do the same with the earth until it is free from all moisture. The moisture that you separated from the earth is the white oil <note>original: 'oleum album'</note>, which is very precious for healing. Because the nature of the "body" <term>body: 'corpus'; the solid, material aspect of the alchemical substance</term> participates more in this oil, it is more difficult to separate from it; consequently, it is considered a more precious thing for completing all works. 

When you see that the earth is between its own weight and light, in the form of a powder, place a small amount of it upon a hot plate of copper. See if any of it evaporates; if it does not, this is a sign that it is stripped and cleansed of all those things that held it in "life" <note>This likely refers to the "volatile" or "organic" impurities that must be "killed" or removed to reach the fixed, eternal state</note>. Keep this substance very dear, for it is the magnesia <term>magnesia: not the modern mineral, but a symbolic name for the "prime matter" or the purified, stable "earth" of the philosophers</term> which will give you what you seek after its resurrection. And when dry...

#### Chapter 20

Son, the third operation of our stone is that you bring back the oil (which is marked by the letter M) <note>M often represents Mercury or the volatile spirit in this manuscript's cipher</note> after its rectification <term>rectification: the process of purifying a liquid by repeated distillation to reach its highest potency</term> together with O, which is brought back with L. Therefore, when the substance is dry and especially void of all its moisture, it most willingly recognizes and "drinks" its own humidity. If you understand us, then understand what we said in our theory in chapter 6.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 52 regarding the final stages of the Great Work. While the previous page focused on the preparation of the metallic powder and the role of quicksilver as a medium for multiplication, this section explains the internal mechanics of "fixation"—how the elemental qualities of fire and dryness transform the flowing mercury into a stable, "fixed" state through the action of alchemical sulfur.</meta>

Fixed water <note>original: "aqua fixa"; a liquid substance that has been made resistant to evaporation by heat</note> is given; its strength is multiplied within the virtues of the nature of Fire. The cause of its dryness is the ending of its warm humidity, which it receives from the compression of the Fire that resides within its own bowels and veins. 

Just as quicksilver <term>Argentum vivum: 'quicksilver' or mercury; viewed by alchemists as the 'mother' of all metals</term> is an overflowing and flowing liquid, it becomes so through the essence of Fire—by means of combustion and burning. From this comes the matter of sulfur, in which Fire is engaged and compressed through the ingenuity of the mastery <term>Magisterium: 'the mastery'; the entire skilled process of the alchemical Great Work</term> with respect to nature, until it is finally limited into a dry and subtle earth. 

This earth is the sulfur; and just as that which is the matter of Fire receives its form from there, so too does quicksilver stand as the matter of sulfur. It is converted into sulfur by reason of the property of its own nature. Therefore, it is not at all wondrous when it is seen to change before your eyes through the power of sulfur. Because sulfur is intensely hot, dry, and full of heat, it must necessarily possess the properties of Fire—namely, the power of opening the parts of the entire body and of the quicksilver, as well as the power of penetrating and "terminating" <note>In alchemy, to 'terminate' a substance means to give it a fixed, solid boundary so it no longer flows or evaporates</note> them. 

Through the heat which it possesses naturally within itself, it opens the closed pores and cuts through the material. When the Fire is thus substantially gathered and constrained into a subtle substance, it enters vaporously into the depths of all bodies. Since the substance by which it is bound and constrained is so very dry, it brings an end to the moisture and congeals <note>original: "congelat"; the process of turning a liquid or vapor into a solid</note> the humidity into which it has entered.

Take a glass vessel with a long neck, one palm <note>A traditional measurement based on the width of the hand, roughly 3 to 4 inches</note> in length, with a round bottom and a mouth so narrow that nothing larger than a middle finger can enter. It should be shaped like the neck of a flask and have a lid that fits inside it perfectly.

Afterward, let an <term>amalgam</term> <note>original: "amalgama"; a mixture of mercury with another metal, used here as a medium for heat and vapor</note> be joined from above. According to our wisdom, place one part of this substance at the bottom of the aforementioned vessel. This must be done in such a way that the stone cannot "breathe"—meaning it should not feel the heat except through the medium of the amalgam.

This is a necessary requirement, for the amalgam of our stone must receive the vapor from the bound essence of the vessel's roundness and be transformed by it. Afterward, place your vessel, sealed with its lid, into the <term>small furnace</term> <note>original: "furnello"</note> among packed <term>coals</term> <note>original: "carbones"</note>. Arrange them so they are separated by roughly the thickness of the back of a knife, so that you can observe the material changing. Then, light the fire, if you have understood these instructions well.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on alchemical "sulfurs," focusing on Chapter 25 which deals with the second sulfur's potency and its numerical multiplication.</meta>

->On the multiplication of Our second sulfur<-
->and its properties<-
### Chapter 25

My son, you must not remain ignorant of the power of all your <term>sulfurs</term> <note>In alchemy, "sulfur" refers not just to the chemical element, but to one of the three primary principles of matter, representing the active, masculine, or combustible soul of a substance.</note>; their power is recognized in two ways: namely, according to the number of degrees of their virtues, and specifically through signs and experiences which testify by the clear light of the senses' reason. My son, the virtues are precisely <note>original: "punctualiter"</note> and gradually bound and glued together through the augmentation and strengthening within the substance of our sulfur. Therefore, we tell you that the more precise degrees it possesses, the greater the power its substance has to <term>transform</term> <note>original: "alterandi," referring to the alchemical process of changing the fundamental nature of a base metal into a noble one.</note> all weighable and double things by any of the ten numbers. This occurs because of the property of the said virtue, which exists in a divine form through which the matter is specified within the substance of the sulfur. If you add more, the succeeding substance receives greater virtue because of the plurality of virtues captured and collected in the aforementioned substance. 

For example, let us suppose that the first sulfur possesses within itself only fifteen precise points in its number of virtues. For this reason, I say that experience teaches us that a sulfur which has only one degree of virtue, in a given weight of simple substance, transforms two whole weights; these are divided by half into ten numbers, which are called and named "simple weights." Not numbered weights, but simple weights: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, ten.

<summary>This chapter explains how the potency of alchemical sulfur is determined by "degrees of virtue" and precise numerical points, which dictate its ability to transmute other substances by weight.</summary>
<keywords>sulfur, multiplication, transmutation, degrees of virtue, alchemical weights, Chapter 25</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 55 regarding the mathematical proportions and numerical power of "sulfurs" in alchemical transformation. It moves from the definition of simple weights to the proportional multiplication of the sulfur's power during the process of "projection."</meta>

The composite numbers, the simple ones, and the number of the 10 wheels of integers—because they receive their relationship from the unit—mean that the gradual power of our <term>sulfur</term> <note>In alchemy, sulfur represents the active, masculine principle of transformation and the "soul" of a substance.</note> corresponds to 10, and so on for the others. Therefore, I understand that through the difference of convertible material weights, and through the <term>intensification and reduction</term> <note>original: "Intentionem et Remissionem." In medieval physics, this referred to the way a quality like heat or spiritual "virtue" could increase or decrease in intensity within a body.</note> of the sulfur's power, the specific power to transform said matter into various forms has been declared in the *Treatise on the Degrees of the Great Medicine* <note>The "Great Medicine" is another name for the Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of the alchemical work.</note>.

Therefore, my son, since one whole virtue <term>congeals</term> <note>original: "Congelat." This refers to the alchemical process of solidifying or "fixing" a liquid substance, like mercury, into a solid, stable metal.</note> exactly 10 parts of <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "argenti vivi," meaning mercury.</note> into perfect metal by the <term>projection</term> <note>Projection is the final stage of the alchemical Great Work, where a small amount of the Elixir or Stone is "thrown" onto a molten base metal to transform it into gold or silver.</note> of one weight upon 10 in substance, it follows that ten weights of 10 virtues congeal 100 parts of quicksilver proportionally into perfect metal. This is because the virtue never decreases due to a reduction in the amount of the agent; rather, it operates according to the proportion of the degree of its action. 

This is just as a degree of hot pepper, in the fourth degree <note>Medieval medicine categorized substances like pepper into four "degrees" of intensity (hot, cold, moist, dry).</note>, perceptibly acts upon its <note>original: "nam" for "naturam" (nature)</note> nature and its parts because of the virtue that exists uniformly throughout the whole <note>original: "subaa" for "substantia" (substance)</note> substance of the pepper. Therefore, the degree of intensity remains the same because, however much substance and virtue there is through continuation, it demonstrates the effect proper to its operation, which depends on its <term>graduality</term> <note>The specific level or "degree" of potency assigned to the substance.</note>. 

And if the weight of one or the other is projected upon less than its proportional weight, it causes in that metal a greater <term>combustion</term> <note>original: "Combustionem." In this context, it suggests an overpowering of the base metal by the sulfur, leading to an intense reaction or "burning" of the substance's impurities.</note> or ignition. Its power and ignited state extend further by digesting, surpassing, and burning, as much as it <note>original: "deit" for "debet" (ought/must)</note> must beyond its proportion.

<summary>This page explains the proportional power of the alchemical sulfur, arguing that its ability to transform quicksilver into perfect metal increases mathematically. It uses the analogy of the "heat" of pepper to explain how the intensity (graduality) of a substance's virtue remains consistent regardless of the amount used.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of alchemical weights and proportions from page 56. While the previous page established that the power of "sulfur" operates on a decimal scale (1 to 10, 10 to 100), this page focuses on the physical manifestation of that power—the "ignition"—and how a small amount of concentrated "virtue" can transform a much larger mass of mercury.</meta>

The weights of the convertible materials <note>Materials capable of being transformed into gold or silver.</note> will achieve the effect of perfect **abstraction** <note>original: "abstractionis perfectae." In alchemy, this refers to the purification or extraction of the pure essence from base matter.</note> so much more effectively if the weight of the materials being converted is increased not just proportionally to the substance, but beyond the proportion of a fixed weight. My son, through this **intensity** <note>original: "intentionem." A term from medieval physics referring to the degree of strength or concentration of a quality like heat or "virtue."</note>, if you do not fully understand, you may grasp the true weight of the virtues of our **Stone** <note>The Philosopher’s Stone, the goal of the alchemist's work.</note> until it reveals to you the power of simple **ignition** <note>original: "ignitionis." This refers to the ability of the alchemical medicine to "fire" or activate the base metal.</note> without hesitation. This is both the limit and the measure: namely, when it **congeals** <note>Solidifies or "fixes" a liquid into a solid state.</note> quicksilver into a perfect metal.

And it releases one of the species of the spirits of gold or silver; and when it releases it in the specific form of **sulfur** <note>original: "sulfuris." Not common brimstone, but the active, masculine principle of alchemy often described as a fine, red or white powder.</note>—that is, as a subtle powder—it then demonstrates that the power of ignition is great. Nor should its power be underestimated because of this; rather, it should be more highly esteemed, since it can reach the absolute limit of ignition through the wise discernment of the operator following the doctrine which, as established above, consists of a **multiplicative substance** <note>A substance that has the power to increase its own potency and the quantity of matter it can transform.</note>.

Therefore, when a great amount of virtue is concentrated in one small subject, it expands and amplifies into a vast mass, bringing it to completion and carrying it to a certain degree of perfection, based on the perfect potential action and the precise weights administered.

Take note, my son, so that you may better understand what we have discussed through a visible demonstration: take **one part of the Stone** and apply it to **ten parts of quicksilver**. Simply **amalgamate** <note>original: "amalgama." To combine mercury with another substance to create a blend.</note> this and observe how the process will conclude.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions regarding the transmutation of quicksilver (mercury). While the previous page discussed the "intensity" and "ignition" of the alchemical sulfur, this page provides specific diagnostic signs for the practitioner to judge the strength of their medicine by observing how it solidifies or "coagulates" the mercury.</meta>

It possesses power <note>original: "virtutem." In this context, "virtue" refers to the inherent active power or medicinal quality of a substance.</note>, and if by itself it does not happen to coagulate quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum." Known today as mercury; its "coagulation" refers to the process of turning the liquid metal into a solid state.</note>, it is of lesser power. In that case, it follows that the weight of the material to be converted must be decreased while the weight of the converting agent is increased. 

But if you leave the quicksilver in the form of earth or a <term>stone</term> <note>original: "lapis." Here referring to the Philosopher's Stone in its solid, medicinal form.</note> in a single mass, its power appears to be **fire-activating** <note>original: "ignificativa." This describes the Stone's ability to "fire" or catalyze the transformation of base metals.</note>. This allows you to recognize that its action is stronger than it was at the beginning. Therefore, it is of greater value; for the more it holds beyond the specific form of a perfect metal <note>Such as gold or silver.</note>, the more power it has over other quicksilver, due to the power of the Stone's virtue diffused within it.

However, if you leave it in the form of a powder, it is a sign that the nobler and stronger potency of its virtue has been diminished. Therefore, take one weight of that powder and apply it to ten weights of quicksilver, as we mentioned above. If it transmutes the mass into a **flower of powder** <note>Likely referring to a very fine, high-quality alchemical precipitate or sublimate.</note>, the excellence of its power should be judged accordingly. 

Therefore, my son, continue this operation until you reach that degree where, at last, it changes quicksilver not just into powder or a perfect metal, but solely into a **white earth** <note>original: "terram albam." In the alchemical "Great Work," the "White Work" (albedo) follows the "Black Work" and signifies the stage where the material is capable of making silver.</note> in the form of a brittle mass. My son, this is our quicksilver.

<summary>This page provides a practical guide for assessing the "virtue" or potency of an alchemical preparation based on its ability to solidify mercury into different forms. It emphasizes that the most powerful state of the medicine is one that transforms mercury into a brittle "white earth" rather than a simple powder.</summary>

<meta>The previous page discussed how the physical form of the alchemical medicine (powder vs. stone) indicates its potency, specifically how a solid mass suggests a stronger "fire-activating" power than a powder. This page continues that technical instruction, focusing on the "simple ignition" required to fix the elements into a permanent, breakable mass without burning them.</meta>

That which is changed is turned into a perpetual, permanent form, possessing the power of **simple ignition** <note>original: "ignitio." In alchemy, this refers to the application of heat to activate the "inner fire" or transformative energy of a substance.</note> balanced with the entirety of its own world. However, when this power <note>original: "virtus." Referring to the inherent active force or medicinal quality of the alchemical agent.</note>, in relation to the aforementioned world in the composition of our medicine, is insufficient—since it does not manifest proportionally except for the conversion of the aforementioned **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum." Known today as mercury; its "termination" refers to the process of fixing the liquid metal into a solid state.</note> into the form of a metal, and not to the completion of its elements (which must be brought to completion within the medicine)—unless this is achieved by other means, namely through subtle ingenuity and a long **decoction** <note>original: "decoctio." A process of prolonged heating or "cooking" the substance to refine and concentrate its essence.</note>. 

For this reason, we maintain that one should not reach the end except through a knowledge <note>original: "scientia." Referring to the secret, practiced knowledge of the alchemical art.</note> that is highly effective in both theory and practice. Nor should the medicine be in the form of a powder at this stage, for the ignition would be excessive, and it would scorch and burn the **simple elements**. When we speak of "simple" elements, we do so to distinguish them from the fermentable substance which we place within the <term>elixir</term> <note>original: "elixir." A potent substance capable of transmuting metals and acting as a universal medicine; "fermentable" refers to its ability to multiply its qualities.</note>. 

We cannot specify the exact time for this to manifest; however, that which is transformed into the form of "earth"—becoming an entirely **breakable mass** <note>original: "massa frangibilis." A solid but brittle state of the alchemical matter, indicating a specific stage of dryness and fixity where the liquid has been "conquered."</note>—is the observable medium of simple, artificial ignition used to create a medicine that is both immediate and preservative of all its elements. 

Therefore, my son, observe your **sulfur** <note>In the alchemical tradition, Sulfur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle of matter, which must be "fixed" to reach the stage of the Stone.</note> until you bring it to the stage of "earth," which we call the breakable mass. By considering the proportions of the whole world, you will know how to convert it, and you will possess its powers in their entirety. When, therefore, you wish to test its powers, do not do so using the entire **stone** <note>original: "lapis." The Philosopher's Stone in its finished, solid form.</note>.

<summary>This page explains the delicate process of "ignition" and "decoction" required to stabilize the medicine into a solid "breakable mass." It warns that if the material is reduced to powder too early, the internal fire will burn the delicate simple elements rather than perfecting them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific laboratory instructions for the "fixation" of the medicine described in Chapter 24. It details the iterative process of grinding, boiling, and sublimating the substance to transform its "volatile" aspects into a "fixed" state that can withstand the fire.</meta>

Grind the whole together in a **mortar** <term>mortarium: a bowl-shaped vessel used for grinding and mixing substances, essential for ensuring the materials "become one body."</term> until the whole becomes one body. Afterward, let it be boiled with the little gum <note>original: "gumetta." This likely refers to a viscous alchemical intermediate or a "gum" produced during the previous stages of the work.</note> with one part of its water, and place the whole thus <note>original: "sy-" likely a truncation of "sic" (thus) or "sub" (under).</note> in a **feverish heat** <note>original: "calore febrile." In alchemy, this refers to a gentle, steady heat similar to the internal temperature of the human body, used to nurture the "embryo" of the Stone.</note> just as you did in the creation of our **Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note>.

Continue this **decoction** <term>decoctionem: the process of boiling a substance in a liquid to extract its essence or change its state.</term> until the water above the matter is congealed. By way of **sublimation** <term>sublimatio: the process of heating a substance until it rises as vapor and collects as a solid at the top of the vessel.</term>, gradually strengthen your fire until, at the last, by the lowering of the fire, that which is **volatile** <note>original: "volatile." Refers to the "spirit" or substance that easily evaporates or escapes when heated.</note> is sublimated. And let them know then that the body is produced and turned into the volatile through the power of the volatile, which carried away the **fixed** thing <note>original: "rem fixam." The "fixed" is the heavy, stable part of the matter that resists fire; here, the volatile part is helping the fixed part to rise.</note>.

Afterward, when it turns cold, take that which has been sublimated and, with another part of its water, place it again over the dregs, grinding and imbibing and breaking it down according to what nature wills. Cook and sublimate, and so from now on continue the said **regimen** <term>regimen: the specific, controlled method of managing the heat and timing of the alchemical process.</term> by digesting with fire until two parts of the water are fixed and congealed, and so that nothing of it can be sublimated.

That is to say, then, repeat the regimen of **fixation** <note>original: "fixationis." The goal of this stage is to make the entire substance stable so that it no longer evaporates when heated.</note> again in such a vessel as that in which you performed the reduction. Nor is there a difference between fixation and reduction regarding the method of procedure, except as to the intention of fixing the substance and not merely hardening it. And therefore your fire must be increased in the fixation; for the moist is fixed in the fire through the fire. And that which will be sublimated must always be held above the fixed matter, remaining there until the whole is fixed, because the aforementioned **sulfur**...

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the "Mastery," focusing on the internal relationship between the fixed sulfur and the volatile mercury. When thus **fixed** <note>original: "fixu[m]." A substance is "fixed" when it has been altered so that it no longer evaporates or changes when exposed to fire.</note> and congealed through its own property, and coagulated by itself, it naturally congeals its own **mercury** <term>mercurium: in alchemy, the "spirit" or volatile liquid principle of matter.</term>. Son, if you know well how to dispose **Nature** <note>The alchemist is often called the "Minister of Nature." He provides the external environment (the vessel and the heat), while Nature performs the actual transformation.</note> from without, she herself will suffice to lead the whole matter to the perfection you require. For she, being very wise by her own instinct, is very concerned through a natural appetite for the love of her body, a concern which has no end. Therefore, **natural motion** is thus declared to you, which is the instrument through which she performs all things. 

Son, if you know how to dispose Nature well from the outside, she will work sufficiently from within until her very last step of perfection. For it is through her motions that she will enter, place, and join the **dry** with the **moist**, and she will continue the moist with the dry so strongly—adhering to the Art with such great unity—that the best and most certain man in the world could not conceive or consider how great is the perfection of Nature. She creates adherence through these motions, which are the closest instruments to her works. 

Wherefore, son, it is thus intellectually revealed to you that, for the sake of completing all the aforementioned motions, **slowness** <note>A common alchemical maxim is "Festina lente" (Hasten slowly). Excessive heat or speed was thought to "burn" the flowers of the work or break the vessel.</note> of preparation must be maintained in our **Mastery** <term>magisterio: the "Great Work" or the chemical process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</term>, so that Nature cannot bypass her motions through an excessive haste, by which she might be hindered by an impediment.

<summary>This page explains that the alchemist's role is to provide the correct external conditions so that Nature can internally join the dry (sulfur) and moist (mercury) principles. ### On the fixation of the air
### Chapter 25

Know, son—for we know that he who is of our Mastery is about to hand down the doctrine through paternal fellowship—this is it: you must strive to bring the said matter, which is not **fusible** <term>fusibilem: capable of being melted or liquefied by heat.</term>, to a perfect **liquefaction** <gloss>melting.</gloss>. Your work must be modeled after the way of Nature through repetition and **softening** <note>original: "mollificationem." This refers to making a hard or brittle substance pliable so it can merge with others.</note>. 

And though the matter may be likewise disordered, let it be made continuous through such **moisture** <note>original: "humiditatem." In alchemy, this is the "radical moisture" or "mercurial water" that allows the solid parts to flow.</note> as mentioned above; indeed, let it have a true moisture that can dwell in the heat of any fire without **evaporating** <gloss>original: "exhalatione."</gloss> or reaching its end. And let this stage be modeled for you, son, as orderly as possible; we tell you that as you commit it to the operation, it does not cease to healthily form one single body with the moisture—that is, the **fusible** part.
